❯ /plugin                                                                       

❯ Build an Exquisite Corpse experience for specs, a pass and play drawing game  
  for 2 to 3 players sharing one pair of glasses or colocated experience. The   
  GAME: Players take turns drawing on a single shared canvas. Each player can   
  only see                                                                      
  a narrow seam of the previous player's drawing, never the whole thing. After  
  the last turn the full canvas is revealed at once.                            
                                                                                
  This is the Surrealist parlour game where you fold the paper before passing   
  it                                                                            
  on. Structurally it is a communication protocol with deliberately restricted  
  bandwidth: each player sends a message to the next through the seam, and the  
  reveal is where you discover how badly it was received. CANVAS GEOMETRY: One  
  shared vertical plane in world space, placed by the first player. The         
  plane is divided into horizontal bands, one per player, stacked top to        
  bottom. All bands are COPLANAR. Do not offset bands in depth. CORE            
  FUNCTIONALITY: When a player takes their turn they see:                       
  - Their own empty band, fully editable.                                       
  - A seam strip showing ONLY the bottom N centimetres of the band above,       
    clipped from the actual stroke geometry. N comes from config.               
  - Nothing else. No earlier bands, no ghost, no outline, no hint, no           
    thumbnail. Clip the seam geometrically. Do not render the full previous     
  band and mask it visually. The first player has no seam. Give them the empty  
  band and a light prompt for what to draw, for example "draw a head" in a      
  figure-oriented session, or leave it fully open.Passing the glasses is where  
  the game can be cheated, so sequence it strictly:                             
                                                                                
  1. Player finishes and dwells DONE.                                           
  2. Canvas is cleared from view IMMEDIATELY, before any pass instruction       
     appears. Nothing from the previous turn may be on screen during the pass.  
  3. A pass screen shows only "Pass to player N" and a READY zone.              
  4. The next player dwells READY, and only then does their band plus seam      
     appear. DRAWING:  - Pinch to draw. Hands are free in this game, so gaze is 
  the wrong input                                                               
     here. Reserve gaze dwell for UI zones only: DONE, READY, UNDO.             
  - Each player gets a distinct stroke colour, assigned automatically. This     
    makes the reveal legible and shows who drew what.                           
  - UNDO is required, at least 10 levels within a turn. Midair pinch-drawing    
    is imprecise and a game without undo is frustrating.                        
  - Strokes clip to the player's own band. A player cannot draw into a          
    neighbouring band. Clip the stroke at the boundary rather than rejecting    
    the whole stroke, so a gesture that drifts slightly does not lose work.     
  - Stroke width fixed. Do not add a brush size control. It is a party game     
    and every extra control is a turn that takes longer. Do not render the      
  canvas plane, the bands, or any backing surface. They are                     
    invisible geometry used for clipping and layout only. The strokes are the   
  only                                                                          
    visible content. Specs is an additive display, so there is no way to render 
  an                                                                            
    opaque sheet convincingly and no way to draw a dark line. Lean into that:   
  the                                                                           
    drawing is emitted light floating in space, not ink on paper.               
     Make the stroke thin enough to get some level of detail. Lines should have 
  a glowing neon look with high emissive settings. REVEAL: After the last       
  turn, reveal all bands at once on the shared plane. Then:                     
  - Let players walk around and view it at full scale. This is the moment the   
    whole game exists for, so give it room. No UI chrome over the drawing.      
  - Offer a replay that draws the bands back on in turn order, so you can see   
    how each person misread the seam.                                           
  - Show the per-player colour key so the group can see who contributed what.   
  - Offer PLAY AGAIN, which keeps the same player count and canvas placement.   
  UI DESIGN: There should be two rounded buttons with white outline and white   
  text: UNDO and DONE, with DONE setting up for the next player or showing the  
  big reveal

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Two design calls I want your read on before I build. First: how should 
     the canvas get placed? → Pinch to place (Recommended)
     · Your spec never mentions audio. House rules here default to adding sound 
     for game-like builds. Which do you want? → SFX + ambient bed
     · For the reveal, how much should the strokes persist between rounds? → 
     Clear everything (Recommended)
   
⏺ Plan locked. Here's what I'm building before I start generating.

  ExquisiteCorpse — tier: interactive

  ┌──────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Piece   │                            Detail                            │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Canvas   │ 120 × 150 cm vertical plane, world-locked, pinch-placed by   │
  │          │ player 1. Invisible — only strokes render.                   │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Bands    │ canvasHeight / playerCount, stacked top→bottom, coplanar. 75 │
  │          │  cm each at 2P, 50 cm at 3P.                                 │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Seam     │ Bottom 6 cm of the band above, geometrically clipped from    │
  │          │ the stroke polylines, not masked.                            │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │          │ Pinch. Ray from head through pinch point → plane             │
  │ Drawing  │ intersection. Strokes are additive neon ribbons built with   │
  │          │ MeshBuilder — bright core + two wider dim halos.             │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Clipping │ Liang-Barsky polyline clip to the band rect. A stroke that   │
  │          │ drifts out gets split, not rejected.                         │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ UNDO /   │ Gaze-dwell, world-locked below the canvas so head-gaze can   │
  │ DONE     │ actually reach them. UIKit buttons underneath, dwell drives  │
  │          │ the trigger.                                                 │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Pass     │ DONE → strokes hidden same frame → then pass panel + READY   │
  │          │ dwell.                                                       │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reveal   │ All bands on, no chrome over the drawing. Colour key +       │
  │          │ REPLAY + PLAY AGAIN on a panel below.                        │
  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

⏺ Project saved. Built and verified end to end in the preview.

  Exquisite Corpse — 11 scripts, 4 UI modules, 6 icons, 4 audio assets.

  Verified working in preview (drove the full loop): 2 players → place canvas →
  draw → DONE → pass → READY → draw → DONE → reveal.

  The three things the spec leans hardest on, and the evidence they work:

  Seam is geometrically clipped, not masked. Player 1's drawing spanned y ∈ [0,
  19.4] cm. The seam mesh handed to player 2 spanned y ∈ [0, 6.7] and its
  x-extent collapsed from ±5.5 to ±0.5 — only the fragment that actually crossed
  into the bottom 6 cm exists as geometry. The screenshot shows a tiny sliver,
  nothing else.

  Pass sequencing can't be cheated. On DONE the scene query showed both band
  meshes enabled: false and only PassPanel enabled. finishTurn() hides strokes
  as its first statement, before the pass UI is touched.

  Neon reads correctly. One additive mesh per band, 5-column ribbon with vertex
  colour fading to transparent at the edges — the gradient is the glow, so a
  whole band is one draw call. Cyan/magenta/lime per player.

  Three real defects the verify pass caught and I fixed:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Bug          │          Symptom it would have shipped          │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ quat.lookAt aligns    │ Every panel faced away, content hidden behind   │
  │ +Z, not −Z            │ its own backing — the setup screen rendered as  │
  │                       │ an empty grey slab                              │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Panels disabled       │ UNDO/DONE/READY never built colliders — logged  │
  │ before their Frame    │ Dwell zone 'done' has no collider. Gaze dwell   │
  │ reached OnStart       │ would have been dead on device                  │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                       │ Misses target-less pinches. Switched to the     │
  │ Polling isTriggering  │ Interactor's own onTriggerStart, which SIK      │
  │ for free-space pinch  │ documents as firing "regardless of if there is  │
  │                       │ a target"                                       │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Two things to flag:
  
  Not verified in preview: the preview harness can't simulate a target-less
  pinch, so canvas-lock-by-pinch-anywhere is untested (it shares the
  now-verified onTriggerStart path). Because "pinch anywhere" also gives players
  nothing to aim at, I made PLACE CANVAS become a LOCK HERE button during
  placement — that path is verified. REPLAY and PLAY AGAIN are wired and their
  buttons have colliders, but I didn't drive them.

  Judgement call: you asked for 2–3 players sharing one pair of glasses, so this
  is single-device pass-and-play — no SyncKit, no colocated networking. If you
  meant multiple pairs of glasses seeing one shared canvas, that's a different
  build and worth saying now.

  Canvas defaults to 120 × 120 cm at 150 cm with a 6 cm seam; all of that plus
  stroke width, glow, dwell time and the three player colours are Inspector
  knobs. The ambient bed you asked for loops at 0.22 volume.

  Next: verify on device | tune the neon/layout | LEAF tests | publish — or tell
  me what to change.

⏺ All three done and verified in preview. Saved.

  Up to six players. Picker is now 2–6. Rather than subdividing a fixed
  canvas (which would have given each of six players a ~20 cm strip), the
  canvas grows: bandHeightCm (34 cm default) × player count. Six players →
  a 120 × 204 cm canvas, confirmed in the scene as six Band_0..5 meshes
  and outline bounds of exactly ±59.85 × ±101.85 cm. Six player colours:
  cyan, magenta, lime, orange, violet, yellow, with all six colour-key
  rows built.

  Band outlines. A thin inset rectangle per band, drawn through the same
  neon ribbon system as one extra mesh. Your active band is brighter than
  the rest. They're on during turns and the reveal, and off during the
  pass screen so the hand-over stays completely clean. showBandOutlines
  and outlineIntensity are Inspector knobs.

  Note this reverses your original "do not render the bands" instruction —
  I took the new ask as the decision.
  
  The UI legibility fix — I found the actual cause rather than just
  nudging sizes. Two compounding bugs:

  - GhostGradients.idleBorder has both gradient stops set to Transparent,
    and while borderType is "Gradient" the borderColor I was setting is
    ignored entirely. So the buttons genuinely had no outline — my earlier
    "white outline" was doing nothing. Fixed by writing a solid-colour
    gradient.
  - The Frame's backing plate was near-opaque grey. On an additive display
    that plate adds light and washes out the white text sitting on it.
    Now panelOpacity (0.35).

  Plus bigger type, brighter secondary text (0.55 → 0.82 alpha), larger
  HUD and buttons, and a chunkier dwell bar. The before/after on the setup
  screen is stark — outlines went from invisible to crisp.

  Two ergonomic fixes that fell out of testing six players:
  
  Problem: Pushing a 204 cm canvas back far enough to fit the Specs FOV
  shrinks each band to ~8° — too small to draw in                
  Fix: Distance is driven by band height, not canvas height. Your band
  stays comfortable; seeing the whole corpse is the reveal's job, which
  is what walking around is for
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Problem: At six bands the canvas bottom is >1 m down, so a HUD below it
  meant craning your neck for every UNDO
  Fix: The HUD now hangs below the active band and follows it down the
  canvas

  Also anchored the canvas top near eye level (topAboveEyeCm), so a 2 m
  canvas doesn't tower overhead — short canvases stay centred.

  One thing I could not verify: driving six full turns through the
  preview. The harness can't simulate a target-less pinch and can't reach
  distant buttons, so I verified turn 1 of 6 end to end plus the
  structural state of all six bands and key rows. The turn→pass→reveal
  sequencing itself was verified earlier and is unchanged.

  Worth a look on device next, since the additive-display legibility is
  the thing you were reacting to and the preview's grey background
  flatters it poorly — panelOpacity and outlineIntensity are the two dials
  if it still reads weak.

⏺ Text is substantially bigger everywhere. Compiles clean, runs clean,
  saved.

  What drove it. The type scale is designed so one constant moves every
  label together, so I raised FONT_SIZE_SCALE from 1.0 to 1.45. That
  baseline is calibrated for 110 cm, but this game places panels at 150
  cm+, which is a lot of why it read small.

  Raising it alone would have cramped or clipped the cells, so three
  things moved with it:

  - Cell heights now derive from the glyph. They were hardcoded (±1.6 cm);
    now roleHalfHeightCm() computes them from the em-square relation, so
    text can't outgrow its cell when the scale changes again.
  - Supporting labels moved up a real step. The scale is compressed
    between 38 and 41 — Body, Caption, Callout and Button are nearly
    identical — so bumping the number alone wouldn't have separated them.
    Prompts, sub-instructions, status and colour-key labels are now
    Headline2; turn label and READY are Headline1; every button label
    defaults to Headline2 instead of Button.
  - Rows, buttons and panels grew to hold it: HUD 48×16 → 54×22 with 19 cm
    buttons, setup 56×32 → 62×42, pass 48×26 → 62×34, reveal 62×28 →
    70×34. Button icons scale with their labels too.

  New knob: each panel has textScale (default 1.0) that multiplies on top
  of the global scale, so you can push any single surface further in the
  Inspector without touching code. It works by scaling the distance passed
  to applyTextRole — mathematically identical to scaling the size, and it
  avoided threading a new argument through every call site.

  I also checked every longest-expected string against its cell budget
  The HUD capture tells the story best: "Draw a head" and "PLAYER 1 OF 4"
  are clearly readable against a busy night-street background, which is a
  far harsher test than the flat grey earlier shots.

  If it still reads small on the actual glasses, raise FONT_SIZE_SCALE in
  ExquisiteCorpseUiCommon.ts — everything tracks from that one number — or
  nudge a single panel with its textScale.

⏺ All three done, verified, saved.

  Prompt removed. firstPlayerPrompt is now blank — player 1 gets an empty
  band and no instruction. I cleared the stored "Draw a head" from the
  scene too, not just the script default. The input is still there if you
  ever want a themed session.

  Active outline thicker and brighter, inactive faded. The ribbon builder
  now takes an optional per-stroke style, so the active band's frame is
  genuinely thicker rather than just brighter. Three knobs on the Stroke
  Renderer:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬─────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Knob           │ Default │             Effect             │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ outlineWidthCm          │ 0.30    │ Active band's outline          │
  │                         │         │ thickness                      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ activeOutlineBrightness │ 0.85    │ Active band's brightness       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │                         │         │ Inactive bands, as a fraction  │
  │ inactiveOutlineAlpha    │ 0.18    │ of both the active brightness  │
  │                         │         │ and thickness                  │
  └─────────────────────────┴─────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  Inactive bands thin out as well as fade, so the band you're drawing in
  is unmistakable at a glance.

  But the real find was why your text still looked wrong. Lens Studio
  serialised every @input into the scene at the original bootstrap, so all
  the panel-size increases I made last turn were being silently ignored 
  at runtime — HudPanel was still running at 40 × 12 cm with 14 cm buttons
  while the script said 54 × 22 / 19. That's why UNDO and DONE were
  spilling out of their capsules: the type grew 1.45× and moved up a role,
  but the containers never actually changed. I pushed the current sizes
  into the scene, and the labels now sit properly inside their buttons.

  Worth knowing for future tuning: editing a default in the script won't 
  move an already-bootstrapped input — change it in the Inspector, or I
  push it to the scene.

  Two related fixes that fell out of testing:

  - The setup menu was inheriting the canvas's vertical drop, so at 4+

  ⎿  Added 54 lines
      128    return out
      129  }
      130  
      131 +/**                                                     
      132 + * Break a polyline into dashes of `dashCm` separated by
          + `gapCm`.                                               
      133 + *                                                      
      134 + * Walks by arc length rather than per-segment so dashes
          + stay an even length                                    
      135 + * across corners — a per-segment approach restarts the 
          +pattern at every vertex                                 
      136 + * and makes rectangle corners look ragged.             
      137 + */                                                     
      138 +export function dashPolyline(points: Vec2Cm[], dashCm: n
          +umber, gapCm: number): Vec2Cm[][] {                     
      139 +  if (points.length < 2 || dashCm <= 0 || gapCm <= 0) re
          +turn points.length ? [points] : []                      
      140 +                                                        
      141 +  const period = dashCm + gapCm                         
      142 +  const out: Vec2Cm[][] = []                            
      143 +  let current: Vec2Cm[] | null = null                   
      144 +  let travelled = 0                                     
      145 +                                                        
      146 +  for (let i = 0; i < points.length - 1; i++) {         
      147 +    const a = points[i]                                 
      148 +    const b = points[i + 1]                             
      149 +    const dx = b.x - a.x                                
      150 +    const dy = b.y - a.y                                
      151 +    const segLen = Math.sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy)         
      152 +    if (segLen < EPS) continue                          
      153 +                                                        
      154 +    let done = 0                                        
      155 +    while (done < segLen) {                             
      156 +      const phase = travelled % period                  
      157 +      const inDash = phase < dashCm                     
      158 +      const remainingInPhase = inDash ? dashCm - phase :
          + period - phase                                         
      159 +      const step = Math.min(remainingInPhase, segLen - d
          +one)                                                    
      160 +                                                        
      161 +      const t0 = done / segLen                          
      162 +      const t1 = (done + step) / segLen                 
      163 +      const p0 = {x: a.x + dx * t0, y: a.y + dy * t0}   
      164 +      const p1 = {x: a.x + dx * t1, y: a.y + dy * t1}   
      165 +                                                        
      166 +      if (inDash) {                                     
      167 +        if (current && samePt(current[current.length - 1
          +], p0)) current.push(p1)                                
      168 +        else {                                          
      169 +          if (current) out.push(current)                
      170 +          current = [p0, p1]                            
      171 +        }                                               
      172 +      } else if (current) {                             
      173 +        out.push(current)                               
      174 +        current = null                                  
      175 +      }                                                 
      176 +                                                        
      177 +      done += step                                      
      178 +      travelled += step                                 
      179 +    }                                                   
      180 +  }                                                     
      181 +  if (current) out.push(current)                        
      182 +  return out                                            
      183 +}                                                       
      184 +                                                        
      185  // ── Ribbon construction ──────────────────────────────
           ────────────────────────
      186  
      187  /** Vertex layout consumed by buildRibbon: position(3) +
            color(4). */

  One thing worth flagging. Laying out the single-line bar exposed that my
  character-width estimate was ~30% too low — the 0.0128 cm/char figure
  in the UIKit notes is for mixed-case body text, but these labels are
  bold caps, which measure ≈0.0166. That's why "PLAYER 1 OF 3" ran
  straight through the UNDO button on the first attempt.

  I re-checked every string against the corrected number and found two
  more overflows I'd otherwise have shipped: the pass screen's
  sub-instruction was 64.6 cm in a 57 cm cell, and the reveal colour-key
  labels were 12.6 in 13. Fixed by widening the bar to 78 cm with 16 cm
  buttons, shortening the pass line to "Then look at READY to begin", and
  widening the key cells. The measured metric is now in the code as
  estimateTextWidthCm() so future sizing isn't guesswork.

  Also removed the now-dead poseBelowBand since the HUD no longer tracks
  the active band.

  Two notes: the reveal's colour key still sits on the panel below the
  canvas — you didn't ask to move it, so I left it. And "Finished!" sits
  left-aligned in the bar, matching where "PLAYER N OF X" sits, rather
  than re-centring when the buttons hide; say the word if you'd rather it
  centred.

  Line style, as redirected. One solid outline around the whole canvas, thin

  so 2–6 still works, it just isn't asked.

  Per-band prompts. "Draw a Head / Draw a Body / Draw Legs" at the bottom centre
  of the band being drawn, from a comma-separated bandPrompts input. It's a new
  ExquisiteCorpseBandPromptUI module with no Frame or backing plate — a plate
  inside the canvas would add light across the drawing surface, and skipping the
  Frame also sidesteps the initialisation ordering the panelled surfaces have
  to work around.

  Smaller splice. Seam 6 cm → 3.5 cm, about 11% of a 32 cm band.
  
  Canvas fits the display. 120 cm wide was 166% of the horizontal FOV. Now 64 ×
  96 cm at 150 cm — 89% horizontal, and I raised topAboveEyeCm to 48 so three
  sections centre on the eye line instead of hanging the bottom band out of
  view.

  Two bugs found along the way, both of which would have shipped silently:

  - The colour key never appeared. setColorKey toggled row.root.getParent(), but
    row.root is the flex cell — so it was disabling the entire row of entries
    rather than one. Every key row read enabled: false in the live scene.
  - The setup panel overflowed the display by 33% and the HUD bar by 8%. I
    checked all five surfaces against the FOV rather than just the two I'd
    changed.

  On your earlier questions, both still stand: REPLAY and NEW GAME are both on
  the end screen (I'd only removed the headline — they were just below the crop
  in that capture), NEW GAME now carries the home icon, and dwell is per-zone
  weighted by consequence — UNDO 1.2 s, READY 1.4 s, DONE 2.1 s, NEW GAME 3.6 s
  — with the label and outline shifting toward a teal tint as the hold builds,
  so a dwell in progress is visible peripherally.

  One loose end worth knowing: the scene still holds orphaned values for the
  renamed outline inputs (outlineWidthCm, activeOutlineBrightness, etc.).

  looked properly. So my "loose end" was an artifact of checking too early —
  sorry for sending you after a non-issue.

  Two things worth keeping from it, though. The audit script is a genuinely
  useful check after any input rename, since a real orphan is invisible at
  runtime. And it caught a flaw in my own tooling: the first pass reported 10
  false orphans on ExquisiteCorpse because my regex only handled the
  decorator-stack form and missed single-line @input canvasController!: Type
  declarations — worth knowing if you reuse that check.

⏺ Home screen now follows the hero: logo mark, "Exquisite" upright, "Corpse" in
  cyan between hairlines, all in Instrument Serif. Saved.

  Font. Imported via the FontSelector tool as Assets/Fonts/Instrument Serif.ttf,
  baked onto each Text via t.font. The tool needs a Text component to target
  and the panel's text is built at runtime, so I created a temporary carrier
  object to hold the import and deleted it afterwards.

  Logo, and one judgement call you should know about. Your artwork is dark navy
  linework on a cream fill — rgb(15,23,39), luma 22. On an additive display
  black is transparent, so blended additively the outlines, neck and legs would
  have disappeared entirely and left a glowing cream blob. That's a hard display
  constraint, not a taste call, so I generated a luminance-flipped variant
  where the ink glows and the "paper" drops to black:

  - CorpseLogoAdditive.png — wired up, linework at luma 232, cyan eye and pale
    crescent preserved
  - CorpseLogoOriginal.png — your art as supplied, also imported

  It's a logoTexture input, so you can swap between them in the Inspector
  without touching code. Both dropped into Assets/Textures/ and Lens Studio
  auto-imported them with .meta files.

  One thing I could not do: the italic. The catalog confirms Instrument Serif
  ships ["regular", "italic"], but FontSelector has no variant parameter — it
  imported only the regular, as a single Font asset rather than a FontFamily, so
  neither t.italic = true nor preferredStyle can reach an italic face. "Corpse"
  is therefore upright, carried by the cyan and the flanking rules instead.
  Fetching the italic TTF from Google Fonts directly would work, but that's an
  outward network request I didn't want to make unasked — say the word and I'll
  grab it.

  Two smaller things: the setup panel grew to 56 × 58 cm (90% of the display
  width, 65% of its height) to hold the lockup, and the accent is now the hero's
  cyan #17AFC5, which also tints the PLACE CANVAS outline.

  The all-caps title persisting through the first attempt was the
  stale-scene-value trap again — titleText and accentColor were still holding
  their bootstrap values and overriding the new script defaults until I wrote
  them into the scene.

  renders large. On the actual glasses 0.4 cm at 130 cm is a fine hairline —

  Monospace choice: Azeret Mono. Geometric and neutral, holds up at small sizes,
  and sits back from the serif rather than competing. It also fixes something
  the proportional face was doing: monospaced digits mean "PLAYER 2 OF 3" no
  longer shifts width as the count changes.

  I applied it as the default for every UI text rather than tagging each site,
  so it covers all of it in one place — every button label (PLACE CANVAS, UNDO,
  DONE, READY, REPLAY, NEW GAME), the status bar, the in-canvas prompts, the
  pass instruction, and the reveal colour key. The title opts back into the
  serif explicitly. If you ever want to swap the working face, THEME_MONO in
  ExquisiteCorpseUiCommon.ts is the single line.

  Three new inputs on the setup panel — straplineText, ruleGapCm, titleLeadingCm
  — so the wording and both spacings are tunable without code.

  One judgement call: I put the strapline at Caption, the smallest role in the
  scale. Monospace runs wider per character than the serif, so at 35 characters
  it's using about 43 of the 51 cm available — going smaller still is fine, but
  shortening the string would be the safer lever if you want more air around it.

⏺ Uppercase restored, and I set the script default to match so the two can't
  drift apart. Saved.

  Why ruleGapCm did nothing. The gap spaces the cells, not the glyphs. The
  "Corpse" cell was a fixed 26 cm while the word only fills ~18, leaving 4 cm of
  dead cell on each side that no gap value could close — at ruleGapCm: 0 you
  were still looking at 8 cm of built-in air. The cell now sizes itself to the
  word via estimateTextWidthCm, so the gap控制s what you'd expect. I left it at
  0.6; it'll respond properly now, and it adapts if you change the word.
  
  Strapline size. Caption is the floor of the type scale, so I added
  straplineScale (0.7) which takes it below the scale's smallest role — that's
  the dial for the relationship to the title.
  
  The two you added mid-flight:
  - buttonLabelScale (0.65) shrinks the PLACE CANVAS label independently of the
    capsule.
  - Corners are properly round now. setVariant({shape: "Capsule"}) was silently
    doing nothing on this theme, so I set cornerRadius directly on the button's
    visual as a fraction of its shorter side — cornerRoundness defaults to 0.5,
    true capsule ends at any button size, and it applies to every NeonButton.

  One correction to something I said earlier: I claimed the capitalisation was a
  font or render issue and pinned capitilizationOverride. That was wrong — the
  runtime string itself was uppercase because the scene value had diverged from
  the script default. That's the same stale-value pattern that's bitten us three
  times now; worth remembering that once an input exists in the scene, editing
  its default in code has no effect.
  
  The capitilizationOverride = None pin I added is harmless and still worth
  keeping — it guarantees text renders exactly as authored rather than
  inheriting a transform.

⏺ The colour palette is in and verified in preview. Project saved.

  What's there

  A palette strip sits below the canvas for the whole of every turn — six chips,
  one per palette slot, pinch to pick. Pinch, not gaze dwell: dwell stays
  reserved for the commit actions (DONE / READY / UNDO) where a slow deliberate
  hold is the point, whereas picking a colour wants to be instant and the hand
  is already free between strokes.

  All six render at full emission, so every colour glows. Selection is signalled
  by a white ring on the active chip rather than by dimming the others, because
  a dimmed swatch stops reading as emissive on an additive display. I also
  raised the stroke glow itself (glowIntensity 1.35 → 1.7, glowSpread 3.4 → 4.2,
  coreWhiteness 0.55 → 0.68), so the drawn marks match the chips.

  A latent bug this surfaced

  The draw controller started a stroke on any pinch, including one aimed at a
  button. Without a fix, every colour pick would have laid down a mark. It now
  ignores pinches that have an Interactable target. Verified in preview:
  pinching a chip moves the ring and draws nothing, and a probe on a free-space
  pinch reported currentInteractable=null startPoint=yes, confirming the guard
  doesn't block real drawing. I couldn't produce a visible stroke through the
  preview harness — its simulated pinch lasts a single frame, so no polyline
  forms — that's a harness limit, not a code path issue.

  One judgement call worth your attention
  
  The reveal colour key can no longer be a strict per-player key: with free
  colour choice, a player can use several colours, and two players can use the
  same one. The key now shows the colour each section opened with
  (firstColorInBand). It's an honest label for "this band started here", but
  it's a real degradation from "player 2 is the pink one". If you'd rather keep
  an unambiguous key, the alternative is to drop the key entirely at reveal and
  let the drawing speak — say the word and I'll switch it.
  verified the in-game colour palette with all colours glowing, and saved the
  project. Next: decide whether to keep the reveal colour key showing each
  section's opening colour or drop it.

  The placement screen is now stripped
     
  The home screen is dismissed entirely when placement begins. What's left is
  the canvas outline, one small uppercase line, and LOCK HERE. The logo, the
  "Exquisite Corpse" lockup, the strapline and the panel backing are all gone.
  It's a new component (ExquisiteCorpsePlacementUI) with no Frame and no backing
  plate at all — on an additive display a plate there would throw light
  straight across the surface you're trying to judge the position of.

  Why it felt gaze-controlled

  You were right about the cause. The canvas pose was recomputed from head yaw
  every frame, so it was welded to where you looked. That's gone. The canvas now
  carries an invisible grab box its own size, with SIK manipulation constrained
  to translation only — tilt and scale are off, since either would break the
  band maths, which assumes a vertical plane of known size. You grab the drawing
  surface itself rather than a separate puck, because at that step the outline
  is the only thing on screen.

  Two details that fell out of it: the canvas re-faces you as you drag, taking
  its facing from the canvas-to-viewer direction rather than head forward, so
  one dragged off to the side still turns toward you instead of sitting edge-on.
  And lockPlacement no longer recomputes the pose — it used to re-aim from your
  head, which would have thrown your drag away and snapped the canvas back in
  front of you. LOCK HERE now commits exactly where you left it, and the grab
  handle is disarmed the moment it does, so a pinch while drawing can't dr
  Called lens-studio 2 times (ctrl+o to expand)

  The home screen is dismissed entirely when placement begins. What's left
  is the canvas outline, one small uppercase line, and LOCK HERE. The

  Why it felt gaze-controlled

  You were right about the cause. The canvas pose was recomputed from head
  yaw every frame, so it was welded to where you looked. That's gone. The
  canvas now carries an invisible grab box its own size, with SIK
  manipulation constrained to translation only — tilt and scale are off,
  since either would break the band maths, which assumes a vertical plane
  of known size. You grab the drawing surface itself rather than a
  separate puck, because at that step the outline is the only thing on
  screen.

  Two details that fell out of it: the canvas re-faces you as you drag,
  taking its facing from the canvas-to-viewer direction rather than head
  forward, so one dragged off to the side still turns toward you instead
  of sitting edge-on. And lockPlacement no longer recomputes the pose — it
  used to re-aim from your head, which would have thrown your drag away
  and snapped the canvas back in front of you. LOCK HERE now commits
  exactly where you left it, and the grab handle is disarmed the moment it
  does, so a pinch while drawing can't drag the canvas.

  One wording change worth your call

  You asked to keep "PINCH TO LOCK IN PLACE", uppercase and smaller. But a
  pinch now drags rather than locks — LOCK HERE does the locking — so
  that line would have been telling players the wrong thing. I set it to
  "PINCH AND DRAG TO POSITION", uppercase, at 0.55 of the type scale. It's
  an @input (instructionText) on PlacementPanel alongside
  instructionScale, so change it in the Inspector if you'd rather have

⏺ Both done and verified end to end. Saved.

  Chrome centred on the canvas

  "PINCH AND DRAG TO POSITION" and LOCK HERE now sit at the canvas centre,
  overlaying it. Since that block has no backing plate, it floats over
  the surface rather than covering it, and it keeps the instruction and
  the button in the same glance as the outline you're positioning.

  Yes, there's an input — but the existing one couldn't do what you wanted

  topAboveEyeCm looks like the placement control, but it's a cap, not an
  offset: it computes min(0, topAboveEye − canvasHeight/2), which at three
  32 cm bands is min(0, 48 − 48) = exactly zero. It only starts biting on
  taller canvases. Anything you set there at three sections would have
  done nothing, which is probably why it felt like there was no control.

  So I added two that always apply, both on CanvasController:

  - verticalOffsetCm (default −6) — height of the whole canvas relative to
    eye level. Everything anchored to the canvas moves with it: status
    bar, palette, prompts, reveal.
  - menuVerticalOffsetCm (default −8) — height of the head-locked menus,
    i.e. the home screen. Separate, because menus sit in front of you
    rather than on the canvas.

  Verified numerically rather than by eye: canvas centre lands at exactly
  −6.000, home panel at −8.000. Worth knowing the preview camera is
  pitched down ~8.5°, so things still look high in the preview panel even
  when they aren't — don't judge the framing from those screenshots.

  One thing the measurements turned up

  I worked out the vertical budget at your live settings (170 cm placement
  — note your scene has 170, the script default is 150). Usable
  half-height is about ±59.5 cm:

  ┌────────┬───────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────┐
  │ offset │ canvas top/bottom │ status bar top │ palette bottom │
  ├────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 0      │ +48 / −48         │ +65.5 ✗        │ −62.2 ✗        │
  ├────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ −6     │ +42 / −54         │ +59.5 ✓        │ −68.2 ✗        │
  ├────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ −12    │ +36 / −60         │ +53.5 ✓        │ −74.2 ✗        │
  └────────┴───────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────────┘

  −6 is what recovers the top you were losing — the status bar, not the
  canvas, was the thing overflowing. But the palette hangs below the field
  of view at every offset here, including 0, so that one predates this
  change and dropping things further makes it worse. That's why I settled
  on −6 rather than the −12 I first tried.

  The stack is about 128 cm of content in 119 cm of view, so no offset
  fixes it. Raising placementDistanceCm to roughly 200 makes everything
  fit (the canvas is fixed cm so it shrinks in angle, while the panels
  scale with distance), or reduce bandHeightCm. Both are your design
  calls, so I left them alone — say the word if you want either, or I can
  move the palette to overlay the canvas bottom the way the placement
  chrome now does.

⏺ Both are Inspector-tunable. Here's where, and I've applied a first pass
  so you can see it and dial from there. Saved.

  Status bar → HudPanel → ExquisiteCorpseHudUI

  ┌───────────────┬─────┬──────┐
  │     Input     │ Was │ Now  │
  ├───────────────┼─────┼──────┤
  │ panelHeightCm │ 11  │ 9    │
  ├───────────────┼─────┼──────┤
  │ textScale     │ 1.0 │ 0.75 │
  ├───────────────┼─────┼──────┤
  │ buttonWidthCm │ 14  │ 14   │
  └───────────────┴─────┴──────┘

  I also widened the textScale slider floor from 0.6 to 0.35 so you can go
  smaller than it previously allowed.

  The height needed a code change to actually come down. The buttons were
  sized panelHeight − 4, so 4 cm of every bar was chrome. I tightened that
  to − 2.5 and dropped the row padding, which takes 2 cm off the bar
  while keeping the buttons at 6.5 cm. That was deliberate: UNDO and DONE
  are gaze-dwell targets, and shrinking them is what makes a dwell fiddly
  to hold. At 6.5 cm they subtend 2.48°, above the 2.08° interaction
  minimum.

  Palette → PalettePanel → ExquisiteCorpsePaletteUI

  panelHeightCm 9 → 6.5. You'd already taken swatchSizeCm down to 4, so
  the panel was carrying about 3 cm of dead padding around chips that no
  longer needed it. I reclaimed it from the padding rather than the chips,
  and left your 4 cm chips alone. There's no text on this panel — its
  height is chip plus padding, nothing else.

  Two things worth knowing

  The global text lever is FONT_SIZE_SCALE in ExquisiteCorpseUiCommon.ts,
  currently 2.4. That multiplies every panel's type, so if text reads too
  big across the whole lens that's the one constant to change — but it'll
  also resize the hero title you've been tuning, which is why I used the
  per-panel textScale instead.

  Your 4 cm palette chips work out to 1.53° at the current 170 cm
  placement, under the 2.08° minimum for a pinch target. They may be
  fiddly to hit on device. You set that value deliberately so I've left
  it, but 6 cm is where they clear the threshold if picking colours turns
  out to be frustrating.
  it, but 6 cm is where they clear the threshold if picking colours turns
  out to be frustrating.

⏺ Matched, and you can see it in the capture — "PLAYER 1 OF 3" now reads
  as a quiet status line next to UNDO and DONE rather than competing with
  them. Saved.

  Why it was so much bigger

  Two compounding reasons, not just one. The status line was using the
  Headline1 role (54) while both of your reference labels use Caption
  (38). On top of that, the other two each apply their own extra shrink —
  the home strapline has straplineScale 0.7, the placement line has
  instructionScale 0.55 — whereas the status had no equivalent and sat at
  full read distance. Together that put it at 1.15° apparent, against
  0.68° and 0.60°.

  What changed

  The status text is now Caption, with its own new input on HudPanel:

  - statusScale (default 0.55) — the direct counterpart to straplineScale
    and instructionScale.

  Measured after the change: placement 0.595°, status 0.595° — an exact
  match. The home strapline is 0.682°, about 13% larger, which is the
  pre-existing gap between your two references rather than anything new;
  raise statusScale to 0.63 if you'd rather it match the home screen
  instead.

  I kept statusScale deliberately independent of the HUD's textScale,
  because textScale also sizes the UNDO and DONE labels — folding them
  together would mean you couldn't shrink the status without shrinking the
  button text with it.

⏺ Both rails are in, top-aligned with the canvas, and verified through a
  full turn to the pass screen. Saved.

  Control rail — right of canvas (HudPanel)

  Vertical, 11 cm wide. Top to bottom: DONE, player indicator, UNDO. All
  text gone, both buttons icon-only circles with the glyph sized off the
  face rather than the old small in-line cap. The player row is the
  group.png head icon with the number beside it.

  New inputs: buttonSizeCm (8), iconScale (1.15), playerIconCm (4),
  playerNumberScale (0.55).

  Palette — left of canvas (PalettePanel)

  Same treatment: vertical stack, 8 cm wide, one chip per slot. I left the
  chips as rounded squares since your circle instruction was about the
  HUD buttons — say if you want them round too.

  Top alignment

  Your mid-flight note caught something I'd have shipped wrong. Both rails
  were already top-aligned by their pose, but their contents were
  vertically centred, so the first button floated down by half the rail's
  slack — the DONE disc sat about 4 cm below the canvas edge. Both columns
  now pack to the top and the rails are sized to their content (25.5 cm
  and 43 cm). Measured: canvas top at 48, DONE disc top at 46.9, first
  chip top at 47.1.

  The white DONE button

  Worth knowing what I actually did here, because "white background with
  black icon" isn't directly possible. Specs is additive — black is absent
  light, so a black tick painted over white would be invisible. I
  generated Assets/Icons/check_filled.png: a white disc with the tick
  knocked out of its alpha. The tick reads as dark because the world shows
  through where the disc doesn't paint. In a bright environment it'll
  read as a light tick rather than a black one. Nothing else looks right
  on this display; the alternative is a plain outlined circle like UNDO.

  One thing I moved on my own

  The rail has no room for a word, so "Finished!" no longer has anywhere
  to live in it. Rather than drop it — you'd asked for it earlier — I
  moved it to plain in-canvas text above the canvas at reveal, and the
  rail hides entirely at that point since there's nothing left to press.

⏺ Done and verified across two turns. Saved.

  down from. Measured in the running lens: player 1 at y = 50.4 (canvas
  top 48 + gap), player 2 at y = 21.9 (band top 16 + gap).

  Size — moved from the Headline2 role down to Caption, and textScale from
  0.8 to 0.7. That's roughly a third smaller. It's deliberately still
  larger than the status and hint labels, since it's the actual
  instruction rather than a hint — textScale on BandPromptLabel is the
  knob if you want it smaller still.

  One thing I adjusted beyond the literal ask

  Placing the prompt at a bare gap above the fold put it directly on top
  of the seam. The seam strip occupies exactly that space — it's the
  bottom 3.5 cm of the previous player's band, showing through — so the
  text would have printed over the sliver of drawing the current player
  needs to see in order to continue it. The prompt now clears the seam
  height before applying the gap, which is the 21.9 rather than 18.4
  above. It still reads as "just above the fold", about 3.5 cm higher than
  a literal reading would put it. Band 0 has no seam and gets no
  clearance, so player 1 is tight to the canvas edge as you asked.

  The gap itself is PROMPT_GAP_CM (2.4) in ExquisiteCorpseMain.ts if you
  want it tighter or looser.

  A bug this surfaced
     
  Testing it, DONE was jumping straight past the pass screen to the next
  player's turn. The cause is the change you just asked for: with READY on
  pinch, a hand still mid-pinch when the pass screen appears has its
  release land on READY, skipping the hand-off — the one moment in the
  game that must not be skippable. DONE completes by gaze dwell, so this
  only bites when someone happens to be pinching at that instant, but it's
  the whole point of the screen.

  READY now refuses to fire for 0.4 s after the screen appears
  (READY_ARM_DELAY_S in ExquisiteCorpsePassUI.ts). Verified both halves:
  the pass screen holds through a DONE, and READY still advances to player
  2 once armed.

  I should be straight about the evidence, though. I first saw the skip
  while pinching DONE directly through the preview harness, which bypasses
  the dwell — and my attempt to reproduce it cleanly was invalidated when
  the lens reset mid-test. So I've fixed a hazard I reasoned through
  rather than one I pinned down with a clean repro. The guard is cheap and
  correct either way, but if you never see the skip on device, that's
  why.

  Called lens-studio 3 times (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The reveal screen is now just the finished corpse, "Finished!" above it,
  and one restart circle top-right. Verified end to end: three turns →
  reveal → restart → fresh player 1 turn. Saved.

  What changed

  The colour key and its whole backing panel are gone. REPLAY is gone, and
  with it the replay phase, replaySeconds, Phase.Replay,
  startReplay/updateReplay, and the showReplayPrefix calls. The one
  remaining button uses the replay icon, is icon-only in a circular
  outline matching the DONE and UNDO circles, and sits in the top-right
  slot beside the canvas — the same spot DONE occupies during a turn.

  Two real bugs this uncovered

  Testing it, restart appeared to skip straight to "PASS TO PLAYER 2". I
  guessed wrong twice about why and had to instrument it. The log settled
  it:

  finishTurn idx=2
  Dwell zone 'playagain' has no collider — it cannot be gazed at.
  playAgain  ← 179ms later, from the same press

  First, the cascade ran the opposite direction from my assumption. It
  wasn't restart bleeding into DONE — it was DONE bleeding into restart:
  the press finishes the last turn, the reveal appears, the restart circle
  materialises under the still-closing pinch, and the release restarts
  the game 179 ms later. Putting restart in DONE's slot is what creates
  this, and it's reachable on device since these buttons take pinches
  whether or not a dwell zone exists. The circle now stays deaf for 0.6 s
  after the reveal appears.

  Second, and worse: gaze dwell on that button never worked at all. With
  no Frame, the UIKit Button builds its collider in a deferred
  Element.onStart that can't run until its object is enabled — so asking
  for the collider in the same tick as show() returned null and the zone
  was silently dropped. Only pinch worked. Registration now happens 0.2 s
  after show, and the warning is gone.

  I also added a 0.5 s arm guard to the HUD rail and a 0.9 s dwell hold at
  turn start while chasing this. Both are sound protection against the
  same class of slot-collision, but I should be straight that neither was
  the actual fault — the RevealUI fix was. I've left them in as
  belt-and-braces rather than unpick them; say the word if you'd rather I
  strip them back.

⏺ It already does — no change needed, and I've now proven it rather than
  just read the code.

  To make the test meaningful I dragged the canvas well off-centre during
  placement (to about x = −52, y = +17) so that any re-placement would
  visibly snap it back in front of the viewer. Then locked, played all
  three turns to the reveal, and pressed restart.

  The DONE button's world position, which is derived from the canvas pose:

  - before the three turns: (-11.38742446899414, 59.23200225830078,
    364.09649658203125)
  - after restart: (-11.38742446899414, 59.23200225830078,
    364.09649658203125)

  Bit-for-bit identical. And the enabled panels after restart are
  HudPanel, BandPromptLabel and PalettePanel — a live turn 1 with the
  rails up. SetupPanel is not enabled at any point.

  The reason it works: playAgain() clears the strokes and calls
  beginTurn(0) directly. It never touches enterSetup(), which only ever
  runs once from onStart(), and nothing anywhere sets placed back to false
  or re-arms the drag handle. So the canvas keeps whatever pose you
  locked in — you go straight back to drawing on the same sheet, in the
  same spot on the wall.

  The screenshot confirms it visually: the canvas is still hanging off to
  the left where I dragged it, with the fresh turn rail beside it.

⏺ Corner brackets are in, marking the active band and moving down with
  each turn. Verified across player 1 and 2. Saved.

  Four L-shaped legs at the band's corners, built as separate polylines so
  nothing joins across the edges. They're brighter than the fold lines
  and canvas outline (1.25 vs 0.7) since these are the "draw here" marker
  rather than passive guides. They clear at the reveal and during
  placement.

  Four new inputs on StrokeRenderer:

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │         Input          │ Default │                               │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ activeCornerArmCm      │ 6       │ leg length                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ activeCornerWidthCm    │ 0.34    │ line weight                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ activeCornerInsetCm    │ 1.4     │ distance inside the band edge │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ activeCornerBrightness │ 1.25    │ glow                          │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  The arm length is clamped to 45% of the band's width and height, so
  cranking it up can't quietly close the brackets back into the full
  outline they exist to avoid.

  On the inset — worth knowing why it's there rather than zero. Band 1
  shares three of its four edges with the canvas outline, so with no inset
  its brackets render exactly underneath it and disappear. Set
  activeCornerInsetCm to 0 and player 1's markers vanish while players 2
  and 3 keep theirs.

  One thing I got wrong on the way

  My first attempt produced nothing, and I initially blamed the inset —
  wrong. The real cause was that my edit setting activeBand in showTurn
  never applied: I'd anchored the patch on a line that had a if
  (!this.canvas) return guard above it, and unlike my other edits that one
  had no assertion, so it failed silently and I didn't notice. The scene
  query is what caught it — ActiveCorners existed but was disabled, which
  pointed straight at activeBand still being −1. I've since confirmed the
  assignment is in place.

⏺ Cyan brackets now sit outside the band, clear of the white outline.
  Checking they follow to band 2.

  Called lens-studio 4 times (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Both done, verified across two turns. Saved.

  The brackets now straddle the band edge from outside, which reads better
  than the inset version — they frame the band rather than sitting inside
  the drawing area. activeCornerInsetCm is replaced by
  activeCornerOutsetCm (default 1.8), positive meaning outward. It's still
  worth keeping above zero: at exactly zero, band 1's brackets land on
  the canvas outline and disappear under it.

  Colour is a new activeCornerColor input, default cyan (0, 0.85, 1,
  0.75). I also dropped the core whiteness for these from 1.0 to 0.2 — the
  white guides push their core toward white, which would have bleached
  the cyan out of the middle of each line and left it visible only in the
  glow either side.

  On "semi-transparent" — worth flagging, because the alpha slider won't
  behave the way you'd expect. Specs is additive: there's no compositing
  against a background, so nothing can be partially see-through in the
  usual sense. Everything is already see-through, and alpha has no effect
  on that. What actually varies is how much light the line emits.

  So I've wired the colour's alpha into brightness (activeCornerBrightness
  * color.a). Pulling the alpha down makes the brackets dimmer and more
  recessive, which is the effect you're after — but it's brightness doing
  the work, not transparency. activeCornerBrightness (1.25) is the other
  half of the same dial if you want to go further.

✻ Crunched for 2m 18s

❯ ok, let's make it closer to the canvas line, so it's touching but still 
  outside of it                                                           

⏺ Tangency here is computable: the bracket's inner edge should meet the
  outline's outer edge, so the outset is half of each line's width added
  together — (0.42 + 0.34) / 2 = 0.38 cm.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/beth.wickerson/Desktop/SPECS_Hackathon3/Assets/Scripts
      python3 - <<'PY'…)
  ⎿  outset -> 0.38 (tangent to the canvas line)
  ⎿  Allowed by auto mode classifier

  Called lens-studio 6 times (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The cyan brackets now sit right against the white line, hugging it from
  outside without overlapping. Saving.

  Called lens-studio (ctrl+o to expand)
  Called lens-studio (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ activeCornerOutsetCm is now 0.38, down from 1.8. Saved.

  That figure isn't eyeballed — it's the tangency point. The bracket's
  inner edge meets the outline's outer edge when the outset equals half of
  each line's width added together: (0.42 canvas outline + 0.34 bracket)
  / 2 = 0.38. So they touch without overlapping.

  I put that formula in the input's hint, since it'll shift if you change
  either line weight later. I also dropped the slider step from 0.1 to
  0.02 — at this scale 0.1 was too coarse to nudge.

  One thing you'll notice: the glow either side of each line extends
  further than the core (1.5× the width), so the two glows do bleed into
  each other slightly even though the solid parts only touch. That's what
  makes them read as joined rather than as two lines that happen to be
  near each other. If you'd rather see daylight between them, 0.6–0.7
  separates the glows.

⏺ Both done, verified by picking a chip and watching the ring move. Saved.

  Rounded colours — the fill was a flat white pixel stretched into a
  square, so the colour stayed square inside a rounded button. It now uses
  a generated SwatchRounded.png whose corner radius is 28% of its size,
  matching the button's cornerRoundness of 0.28, so fill and outline
  agree.

  Thicker selection outline — this needed a different approach than I
  expected. The button's own border can't be thickened from outside:
  RoundedRectangleVisual keeps borderSize private, and even if it were
  reachable, the visual re-applies its per-state border on every hover and
  press, so any thickness set externally would be wiped the first time
  you moused over a chip.

  So the selection marker is now a generated ring texture (SwatchRing.png)
  that I draw myself, at roughly 7% of the swatch — noticeably heavier
  than the old hairline. It sits 0.9 cm proud of the chip and is simply
  toggled on the selected one. Being an ordinary additive image, nothing
  in UIKit's state machine can overwrite it.

  I also turned the button's own thin outline off entirely on swatches.
  With both present you'd get two concentric outlines a fraction apart,
  which reads as a rendering fault rather than a design.

  Two knobs if you want to tune it: selectionRingBleedCm in the NeonButton
  options controls how far the ring stands off the chip, and the ring's
  weight is the outline_px=36 value baked into the texture — tell me a
  thickness and I'll regenerate it.

  The saving came from padding, not from the controls — the buttons are

  The saving came from padding, not from the controls — the buttons are
  still 8 cm and the chips still 6 cm, so nothing got harder to hit. The
  HUD was carrying 3 cm of chrome around an 8 cm button; it's now 1 cm. I
  also narrowed the player-number cell from 3.2 to 2.6 cm, since it only
  ever holds a digit or two, which is what let the rail come in without
  cramping that row.

  I gave the HUD the same treatment as the palette: panelWidthCm is now
  derived — max(buttonSize, playerRow) + 1 — rather than an input. That's
  one fewer number to keep in step, and it's the same trap that made
  swatchSizeCm look broken earlier.

  The honest limit: width is now set almost entirely by the button and
  chip sizes, and those are near the floor. At 6 cm the chips subtend
  2.29° against a 2.08° minimum for a pinch target; the 8 cm dwell buttons
  have a little more room. So if you want the rails materially narrower,
  it means smaller targets and a real accuracy cost. buttonSizeCm and
  swatchSizeCm are the dials, and the rails will now follow them
  automatically — but I'd stop around 7 cm buttons and 5.5 cm chips.

  If it's horizontal space rather than panel width that's the issue,
  RAIL_GAP_CM in ExquisiteCorpseMain.ts (currently 3) sets how far each
  rail sits off the canvas edge. Pulling that to 1.5 buys 3 cm across the
  layout without touching any target.

  The roots were near-symmetric (−36.30 / +36.69), so the panels weren't
  misplaced — each Frame draws its content off-centre. Relative to its own
  origin a rail's subtree spans −1.1 to +7.2 cm, shifted ~3 cm toward +x.
  Since that shift is the same world direction on both sides, it eats the
  left rail's clearance and pads the right rail's. Exactly the asymmetry
  you described, and my symmetric formula could never have caught it.

  There's now a railContentOffsetCm input on the ExquisiteCorpse object
  (2.65) that cancels it. After the fix:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────┬───────────┐

  ├────────────────────┼────────┼───────────┤

  │ HUD left edge      │ +32.56 │ 2.70 cm   │
  └────────────────────┴────────┴───────────┘

  Both clear, and near-symmetric now.

  One thing that fell out of the measurement: the corner brackets reach
  ±29.86, wider than the canvas outline at ±29.03, because they sit
  outside the band edge. They're the widest thing on the canvas, so that
  2.3–2.7 cm is the real clearance — less than the nominal railGapCm of 4,
  which is measured from canvasWidth/2 = 28. If you want a specific
  visual gap, budget about 1.9 cm for the brackets on top of it.

  I should have measured bounds three rounds ago rather than reasoning
  from the formula — you'd have had this fixed without the detour through
  padding and plate widths.

※ recap: We're building the Exquisite Corpse drawing lens for Specs and just
  finished stopping the HUD rail from overlapping the canvas. Everything

❯ I want to chat about possibilities. Don't change anything yet. This lens      
  needs a wow factor. Idea 1: Make it come alive. if the strokes have a subtle  
  idle motion, a slow breathing wobble along the tube path, the creature the    
  group accidentally built stands up and looks back at you. That's the          
  Surrealist joke completing itself, and it's the difference between "we made a 
  funny drawing" and "we made a thing." Cheap too, since it's a vertex offset   
  with a noise function. Idea 2: animate the canvas folding along the lines, so 
  when you click done, it folds up and the text person only sees the section    
  they are working on (with a slice of the 1st or 2nd drawing) Idea 3:          
  Colocation functionality. Is it possible to play with multiple people wearing 
  specs rather than pass-to-play. Idea 4: Make the canvas look more like white  
  drawing paper, add more palatte colors and bruch sizes. Chat with me about    
  these things, don't implement anything                                        
  
⏺ Good set. Let me give you a real read on each, including a couple of places
  where this codebase makes things cheaper or more expensive than you'd expect.

  Idea 1 — Idle motion
     
  You're right that it's cheap, but not quite for the reason you said. A vertex
  shader would need replacing vertexBaseColorMaterial with a custom graph that
  preserves additive blending and per-vertex colour — doable but fiddly. The
  cheaper route is that rebuildRibbonMesh already rebuilds from polylines, so a
  CPU wobble is maybe 30 lines: offset each point along the segment normal by
  sin(t + arcLength × k).

  The catch is where you do it. During a turn, strokes are clipped to band edges
  by Liang–Barsky at build time. Wobble them and strokes near a fold visibly
  cross the line — breaking the one rule the whole game rests on. It'd also pull
  focus while someone's drawing, and jiggle the seam they're tracing from.

  So: wobble only at reveal. No clipping constraint is being enforced visually
  then, nothing else is happening so you can afford the rebuild, and it becomes
  a moment rather than ambient noise.

  The "looks back at you" half is separate and nearly free —
  faceViewerRotation() already exists. A slow lean tracking the viewer at reveal
  would sell "it's aware of you" more than the wobble does. I'd do both, but
  the lean first if you only pick one.

⏺ Good set. Let me give you a real read on each, including a couple of places where this codebase makes things cheaper or more expensive than you'd expect.

  Idea 1 — Idle motion

  You're right that it's cheap, but not quite for the reason you said. A vertex shader would need replacing vertexBaseColorMaterial with a custom graph that preserves additive blending and per-vertex colour — doable but fiddly. The cheaper
  route is that rebuildRibbonMesh already rebuilds from polylines, so a CPU wobble is maybe 30 lines: offset each point along the segment normal by sin(t + arcLength × k).

  The catch is where you do it. During a turn, strokes are clipped to band edges by Liang–Barsky at build time. Wobble them and strokes near a fold visibly cross the line — breaking the one rule the whole game rests on. It'd also pull focus

  So: wobble only at reveal. No clipping constraint is being enforced visually then, nothing else is happening so you can afford the rebuild, and it becomes a moment rather than ambient noise.

⏺ Good set. Let me give you a real read on each, including a couple of places where this codebase makes things cheaper
  or more expensive than you'd expect.

  Idea 1 — Idle motion

  You're right that it's cheap, but not quite for the reason you said. A vertex shader would need replacing
  vertexBaseColorMaterial with a custom graph that preserves additive blending and per-vertex colour — doable but
  fiddly. The cheaper route is that rebuildRibbonMesh already rebuilds from polylines, so a CPU wobble is maybe 30
  lines: offset each point along the segment normal by sin(t + arcLength × k).

  The catch is where you do it. During a turn, strokes are clipped to band edges by Liang–Barsky at build time. Wobble
  them and strokes near a fold visibly cross the line — breaking the one rule the whole game rests on. It'd also pull
  focus while someone's drawing, and jiggle the seam they're tracing from.

  So: wobble only at reveal. No clipping constraint is being enforced visually then, nothing else is happening so you
  can afford the rebuild, and it becomes a moment rather than ambient noise.

  The "looks back at you" half is separate and nearly free — faceViewerRotation() already exists. A slow lean tracking
  the viewer at reveal would sell "it's aware of you" more than the wobble does. I'd do both, but the lean first if you
  only pick one.

  Idea 2 — Folding

  Strongest idea of the four. It's literally the game's name, and it fixes a real weakness: right now concealment is
  "we hide it," which works but is inert. A fold shows you the rule happening — nobody needs telling.

  Cost is a genuine refactor though. Strokes are baked into world space via toWorld(), so bands can't move
  independently. You'd restructure so each band is a child pivot with band-local points — rebuildRibbonMesh already
  takes a project function, so that's the hook — then folding is just rotating each pivot about its top edge. Moderate,
  not enormous.

  One caveat: with no visible sheet, you're folding strokes in mid-air. That might be more striking than paper — the
  drawing peeling away from you — but it's worth prototyping before committing, because it could equally read as a
  glitch.

  Idea 3 — Colocation

  Technically supported (Sync Kit, colocated anchors), but it's by far the biggest job and it changes what the game is.

  The problem is that exquisite corpse runs on concealment, and Sync Kit's default job is making everyone see the same
  thing. You'd need per-user visibility — each band rendered only on its author's device plus the seam. Achievable, but
  you're working against the framework's grain, and you need multiple devices to test anything.

  The version I'd actually argue for: colocated but still turn-based. Everyone shares the canvas position, each person
  only ever sees their own band, and "pass to player 2" becomes "your turn" appearing in player 2's view. Keeps the
  game intact, kills the handoff friction, no glasses-swapping. That's a v2 feature though, not a hackathon addition.

  Also worth saying: the physical handoff is currently a feature. The ritual and the comedy of passing the glasses is
  part of the fun you'd be trading away.

  Idea 4 — Split verdict

  More colours: yes, easy — but there's a layout cost you'll hit immediately. The palette is a vertical rail that now
  auto-sizes to chip count. Ten chips at 6 cm is a 60 cm+ column, well past the vertical FOV. You'd need two columns or
  smaller chips, and chips are already near the 2.08° pinch-target floor.

  Brush sizes: easier than you'd think. rebuildRibbonMesh already accepts a per-stroke styleFor hook, so varying
  coreHalfCm per stroke is nearly free at the geometry layer. The whole cost is UI real estate and the fact that the
  original spec deliberately excluded it to keep drawings consistent.

  Paper: this one fights the hardware. Additive display means you only ever add light — a white sheet becomes a glowing
  rectangle that washes out the room and, worse, washes out the strokes on it, since they'd have to out-shine it. This
  project has already hit that wall twice: it's why DONE is a knockout icon and why the original spec forbade a
  backing surface. The most you can get is a very faint warm tint, or paper suggested only at the corners. Honestly I
  think the neon-on-nothing look is more distinctive than fake paper would be.

  What I'd do

  Idea 2 for the pass, Idea 1 for the reveal. They bookend each other: the paper folds away from you three times, then
  unfolds all at once into something breathing that turns to look at you. That's one coherent piece of theatre rather
  than two unrelated effects, and it makes both key moments physical.

  If time is tight, Idea 1 alone is the best ratio — reveal wobble plus the viewer lean is a day's work against a
  refactor for the fold.

  Which direction appeals? I'd also want to know whether "wow" here means a demo moment for an audience, or something
  that holds up on the tenth play — that changes whether I'd push the fold or the creature.

✻ Crunched for 1m 38s

❯ Idea 2 for the pass, Idea 1 for the reveal. I would also like for the reveal, the paper unfolds and then fades away. 
  Then the wobbling and drawing looking at you begins. If there is any kind of face detection we can do on the         
  drawing that would be so amazing. The face detection could make the eyes animate or mouth even                       
  
⏺ Good — and the unfold-then-fade ordering solves something. Since paper can't be opaque on this display, the guide 
  lines are the paper: the canvas outline, the dashed folds, the corner brackets. Unfold those, fade them out, and
  what's left floating is just the drawing. That's honest to the hardware instead of fighting it, and it uses meshes
  that already exist with a brightness parameter already wired.

  Face detection — don't use ML, use the stroke data

  An ML face detector would almost certainly fail here. They're trained on photographic faces; a few glowing polylines
  on a transparent background is nothing like their training distribution. You'd also have to render to a texture
  first.

  But you don't need one, because you have the vector data. bandStrokes[0] is the head band's strokes as point arrays
  in canvas-local cm. Per stroke you can cheaply compute bounding box, centroid, arc length, and closure (how near the
  end returns to the start). Then:

  - Eyes = two strokes that are closed-ish, small, similar in area, roughly level in y, separated in x by one to three
    times their own width, in the upper half of the band.
  - Mouth = a stroke below the eye midpoint with a wide, short bounding box.

  That's a few dozen lines of pure maths, no model, no inference cost. And you have an unusually strong prior to lean
  on: the band is literally prompted "Draw a Head."

  The blink is the whole thing. A drawing that wobbles is pleasant; a drawing that blinks is alive. And it's the
  cheapest animation available — hide two small meshes for ~120 ms at irregular intervals. The mouth can open and close
  by scaling that one stroke vertically about its centroid.

  The renderer change is modest: band 0 currently draws as a single mesh, so you'd split the detected face parts into
  their own meshes to animate them independently. rebuildRibbonMesh already takes an arbitrary stroke array, so it's a
  matter of passing three subsets instead of one.

  The failure mode is a feature
  
  Detection will misfire — someone's spiral becomes an eye, two nostrils get read as a pair. I think that's good. The
  entire joke of exquisite corpse is the accident, and a confidently wrong face blinking at you is funnier than a
  correct one.

  The one genuinely bad case is detecting nothing, leaving the reveal without its payoff. So the wobble and the lean
  have to be satisfying on their own, with the face as a bonus layer — never the thing the moment depends on.

  Sequencing — there's a fast path

  Worth knowing: the fold and the unfold need the same refactor. Strokes are baked in world space, so bands can't move
  independently until each becomes a local pivot. That refactor gates both.

  But the creature doesn't need it. Wobble is point offsets, lean is a canvas rotation, face detection is maths on
  existing data. So:

  Phase 1 (no refactor): wobble + viewer lean + face detection with blink. You'd see the creature come alive quickly,
  and it's fully testable on its own.

  Phase 2 (the refactor): band-local pivots, then fold on pass, unfold on reveal, guides fade.

  That order also de-risks things — if the fold prototype turns out to read as a glitch rather than paper, you've
  already got the memorable moment banked.

  Rough beat sheet for the finished reveal, so we're agreed on timing: unfold ~1.2 s → guides fade ~0.8 s → wobble
  ramps in over ~1.5 s → lean engages → first blink about a second later, then irregular. That delay before the first
  blink matters — it should land after you've registered the creature, so it reads as a reaction to you rather than an
  idle loop.

  Want me to start on Phase 1? And is this for a demo in front of an audience, or something that has to survive repeat
  plays? If it's a demo I'd bias the wobble and blink larger than feels right up close, because they'll read as subtle
  from across a room.

  What happens now at the reveal: guides fade over 0.8 s → half-second pause → the drawing starts breathing and leaning toward you. Verified in the running lens: reveal at 20:24:10.9, life at 20:24:12.2 — 1.27 s apart, matching the configured

  Face detection. No ML — it works off the vector data. Per stroke it computes bounding box, arc length and closure (how near the end returns to the start), then looks for two closed-ish, similarly-sized, roughly level blobs in the upper band,
  and a flat stroke beneath them.

  I tested it against nine synthetic drawings rather than guessing, which caught a real bug: with "widest flat stroke below the eyes" a big horizontal scribble beat the actual mouth. It now scores candidates against the eyes — a mouth is about
  as wide as they are apart, sits under their midpoint, and is flat. All nine cases pass now, including square eyes, eyes-with-no-mouth, one-eye, and degenerate two-point strokes.

  One judgement call: I made the mouth require eyes. A lone stroke pulsing open and shut with no face around it reads as a glitch rather than the good kind of accident.

  Tunables, all biased large for across-the-room legibility as you asked:

  - StrokeRenderer: breathAmplitudeCm 0.85, breathWavelengthCm 26, breathSpeed 0.28, mouthOpenAmount 0.7, blinkIntervalSeconds 3.2, blinkHoldSeconds 0.14
  - Main: paperFadeSeconds 0.8, lifeDelaySeconds 0.5, leanStrength 0.35, leanMaxDegrees 14

  Lean is deliberately a fraction of the true angle and clamped — tracking you exactly would read as a billboard following you around rather than something noticing you.

  Two caveats. I couldn't verify the wobble or blink visually, because the preview harness can't lay down strokes (its simulated pinch lasts a single frame), so the reveal I drove had an empty canvas and correctly reported "No face found — body
  only". You'll see the actual motion the first time you draw and finish a real round. And the per-frame mesh rebuild is only affordable because nothing else is happening at the reveal — if it's heavy on device, breathSpeed isn't the lever,
  rebuilding every other frame is.

  Worth a real playthrough before I start Phase 2 — the fold needs the band-local refactor, so it's better to know this layer feels right first.
