⏺ Reworked. Saved.

  The change in model. The sway is no longer anything per-stroke or per-band — it's one continuous function of height across the whole canvas. Amplitude grows from the feet to the head, so the creature is rooted at the bottom and loosest at the
  top, like something standing up. Every point at the same height moves identically, whatever stroke or band it belongs to, so it reads as one body rather than three drawings twitching.

  That also fixed a problem I'd have introduced with any per-band transform: because displacement varies smoothly with y, the bands cannot pull apart at the folds. I verified this — two strokes meeting at a fold line stay joined to 0.00e+0.

  Per body part, exactly as you described:

  ┌───────┬────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       │          sway          │      individual stroke ripple       │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ head  │ most (top of the body) │ 0 — rigid, so it sways as one piece │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ torso │ mid                    │ 0.55×                               │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ legs  │ least (rooted)         │ 1.0×                                │
  └───────┴────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  Head ripple at zero is deliberate: it's what makes the head read as a solid thing swaying rather than a bundle of wobbling lines, and it keeps the face locked together. The eyes and mouth are animated with the head's exact parameters, so they
  can't drift off it.

  Five checks passed on the maths: folds stay joined, amplitude increases with height (0.19 / 0.34 / 2.03 cm at feet / middle / head), the head moves as one piece (delta 8.9e-16 between separate strokes at matching heights), ripple does
  reintroduce per-stroke variation on the legs, and nothing accumulates over 40 s of runtime.

  New tunables on StrokeRenderer: swayBaseCm 0.45, swayTopCm 2.2, swaySpeed 0.16, swayPhaseSpread 1.3 (phase lag between feet and head, so the sway travels up rather than moving rigidly), plus headRipple / torsoRipple / legsRipple multipliers
  over breathAmplitudeCm 0.7.

  Same caveat as before — I still can't see the motion, since the preview harness can't lay down strokes. swayTopCm is the dial if the head reads too still or too loose on a real playthrough.

⏺ Fixed. Saved.

  The cause was mine. The sway is sin(time × speed + height × phaseSpread), and at time = 0 that's already sin(1.3) at the head — about 96% of peak. So the drawing's first animated frame was displaced ~1.7 cm from where it had just been
  sitting. It never eased in; it teleported to mid-swing and carried on from there.

  The movement now ramps up from a standstill over lifeRampSeconds (1.6), using smoothstep rather than a linear fade — smoothstep leaves rest with zero velocity, so there's no kick at the start of the ramp either. Measured: first frame now
  deviates 0.0000 cm from the static drawing, and the worst frame-to-frame step across the whole ramp is 0.04 cm, against 1.74 cm before.

  The ramp covers the ripple and the mouth as well, not just the sway, so nothing else pops in at full strength. The first blink sits at ~3.2 s, comfortably past the ramp, so it still lands as a reaction once the creature has settled.

  lifeRampSeconds is on StrokeRenderer if you want the wake-up slower or snappier — though at this point I'd trust your eye on a real playthrough over my numbers, since I still can't see the motion from here.

⏺ Done. Saved.

  How it finds the outline: the biggest closed-ish stroke in the head band that isn't already claimed as an eye or mouth. Then every other head stroke is ray-cast against it point by point, and if the majority of its points fall outside, it's
  classified as hair. Majority rather than all, so a strand that crosses the boundary still counts.

  Those strokes now render in their own mesh with hairRipple (1.6) — the loosest thing in the drawing, above the legs at 1.0. They still share the body-wide sway, so hair moves with the head while wriggling more than it.

  Tested on a synthetic head — outline, two eyes, mouth, three strands off the top. It picked all four categories correctly, left a stroke fully inside the outline alone, and when there's no outline at all it flags nothing as hair rather than
  guessing.

  Your instinct that it doesn't matter if it's wrong is what makes this safe. There's no failure mode worse than "some strokes wiggle a bit more" — and given exquisite corpse heads tend to be a blob with things sticking out of it, it'll be
  right more often than not.

  The debug log now reports the hair count alongside the face result, so if you turn debugPhases on you'll see Face found, hair strokes: 3 and can tell at a glance whether it latched onto anything.

⏺ Matched, and this turned up the root cause of everything I've been patching around. Saved.

  The real bug. A UIKit Frame doesn't centre its content on its plate — it pins the content a fixed 0.979 cm from the plate's left edge, identical on both panels despite their different widths. So the plate hung off to the right while the
  buttons overhung to the left, and the pair covered far more ground than either alone:

  ┌────────────────┬─────────┬─────────┐
  │ visible spread │ before  │  after  │
  ├────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ HUD            │ 9.98 cm │ 6.94 cm │
  ├────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ palette        │ 9.11 cm │ 7.07 cm │
  └────────────────┴─────────┴─────────┘

  The content is now shifted by half the plate minus that inset, so it sits centred and the visible spread equals the plate width. The two rails are within 0.12 cm of each other, and I dropped buttonSizeCm 7 → 6 so the HUD's control matches the
  palette's chip.

  This also explains the last several rounds. That off-centre content is exactly why the left rail kept eating into the canvas while the right one floated away, and why "shrink the panel" never seemed to help. I'd been compensating with two
  fudge factors — railContentOffsetCm (2.65) and hudRailNudgeCm (3.5) — that pushed whole panels around to hide a layout fault inside them. Both are now 0 and the symmetric positioning works on its own.

  Clearance held up: HUD sits 4.32 cm clear of the corner brackets, plate tops still on the canvas line at 42.53 / 42.42 against the canvas at 42.53.

  A 6 cm button is 2.29° at this distance against the 2.08° minimum, so still comfortably hittable — but that's now the floor if you want them smaller again.

⏺ Two useful facts before changing anything: swatchSizeCm is 6 with a slider floor of 2, so you're not clamped there — but 6 cm is the practical floor for a pinch target at your 170 cm placement (2.29° against a 2.08° minimum; 5 cm drops to
  1.91°). What you are at the floor of is extraWidthCm, now 0.

  So the way to get smaller chips is to separate what's drawn from what's hittable.

⏺ Chips are smaller and the rail narrowed with them. Saved.

  How — I split the drawn chip from the hit area, because 6 cm was genuinely the floor for a reliable pinch and shrinking past it would have made colour picking unreliable. A swatch's button outline isn't drawn, so the collider can stay big
  while the colour reads small:

  - swatchSizeCm (now 4) — the visible chip, and what the rail width follows
  - minTouchSizeCm (6) — invisible hit area, unchanged

  The chip gap is now auto-clamped to at least touch − visible, otherwise neighbouring colliders would overlap and a pinch would land on whichever was found first. So spacing looks after itself as you shrink the chips.

  Net: rail width ~4.3 cm (was 6.35), height ~34 cm (was ~45), hit accuracy unchanged.

  ---

  On the brush control — that freed vertical room, and here's the constraint to decide against. Every control needs a ~6 cm hit area, but the rail is only ~4.3 cm wide. So stacked vertically works, side by side doesn't — two buttons abreast
  would need 12 cm of width.

  Two options that fit:

  A — stacked stepper. ▲ / brush indicator / ▼ running down under the chips. Each drawn small (~2.5 cm) with 6 cm hit areas, auto-spaced. Costs about 15 cm of height, taking the rail to ~50 cm against 84 cm of canvas. Closest to what you
  described.

  B — single cycling button. One chip-sized button showing a dot at the current stroke width; each pinch steps to the next size and wraps. Costs ~6 cm of height, one hit target, no arrows.

  In both, the indicator should be a dot drawn at the actual stroke width rather than a brush glyph — you'd see the size rather than reading it, and it reuses the swatch image machinery.

  The plumbing is already there either way: rebuildRibbonMesh takes a per-stroke styleFor hook, so varying coreHalfCm per stroke needs no geometry work.

  Which shape do you want? I'd lean B for a demo — one target is far more reliable to hit than three small ones, and there's no wrong direction to press.

⏺ Done. The selected chip now sits in a solid white surround rather than a hairline. Saved.

  Why a surround rather than a fill. A white pad behind the chip would have been the obvious reading of "fill", but it doesn't work here — additive rendering means the pad's light adds to the chip's wherever they overlap, so the colour would
  wash toward white exactly where you most need to read it. Same constraint that shaped the DONE knockout icon. A thick band around the chip gets the visual weight you're after without touching the colour.

  I regenerated SwatchRing.png with the band at 17.6% of the texture, up from 7%, and widened selectionRingBleedCm 0.5 → 1.6 so the band has room to sit outside the chip rather than creeping over it. At your 4 cm chips that's a band roughly 1
  cm thick, spanning 1.82–2.80 cm from centre with the chip edge at 2.00 — so it just laps the edge with no gap.

  Two knobs if it needs tuning: selectionRingBleedCm moves the band in or out, and the thickness itself is baked into the texture — tell me a percentage and I'll regenerate it.

⏺ Changed to a real lean. Saved.

  What was actually wrong. The old motion displaced points sideways in proportion to their height, which sounds like a lean but is a shear — the head slid across while the feet stayed put and the body silently stretched. Nothing ever rotated.
  It's now a rigid rotation about a pivot at the foot of the canvas, so the creature tips as one piece the way a standing body does.

  Measured on a body from foot to head at 1.2°:

  ┌──────────────┬──────────┐
  │    height    │  travel  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ feet (pivot) │ 0.000 cm │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ lower        │ 0.412 cm │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ upper        │ 0.824 cm │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ head         │ 1.236 cm │
  └──────────────┴──────────┘

  Linearly increasing from a planted foot — and pairwise distances hold to 7e-15, so the shape is genuinely rigid rather than distorting. A wide body also dips on one side as it rises on the other, which is the giveaway of a true rotation and
  something the old shear could never do.

  Being a rigid transform, it also can't pull the bands apart at the folds — every point moves under the same rotation, so that's now structural rather than something I had to engineer.

  Controls on StrokeRenderer: tiltDegrees (1.2 — about 1.8 cm of head travel over your 84 cm body), tiltSpeed (0.16, one full back-and-forth per cycle so low reads as slow breathing), lifeRampSeconds (1.6, still easing up from a standstill so

  something the old shear could never do.

  Being a rigid transform, it also can't pull the bands apart at the folds — every point moves under the same rotation, so that's now structural rather than something I had to engineer.

  Controls on StrokeRenderer: tiltDegrees (1.2 — about 1.8 cm of head travel over your 84 cm body), tiltSpeed (0.16, one full back-and-forth per cycle so low reads as slow breathing), lifeRampSeconds (1.6, still easing up from a standstill so
  there's no jump on the first frame).

  swayBaseCm, swayTopCm and swayPhaseSpread are gone — the phase spread in particular was making the wave travel up the body, which is a bend and works against a rigid tilt. The per-stroke ripple is untouched: head still rigid, torso 0.55×,
  legs 1.0×, hair 1.6×.

  — leaving it behind you or off to one side. Refresh works because by then tracking is already up, which is exactly the asymmetry you described.

  forever — the panel is world-locked and draggable, and one that followed your head permanently could never be put down.

  The caveat. I can't reproduce a cold project open from here; every tool I have restarts the lens the same way your refresh does, which is the path that already worked. So I've fixed the most likely cause by reasoning about the timing, not by
  watching it fail. Verified what I could: the panel lands 145 cm directly in front of the camera and renders correctly.

❯ is there a way to share the finished drawing on snapchat                                                                                                                                                                                          

⏺ Let me check rather than guess — this is exactly the kind of platform question where I could be confidently wrong.

  Called lens-studio 3 times (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That's the answer, and it's a definite one.

  Not from inside the Lens. The Spectacles compatibility list explicitly names SnapImageCaptureEvent, SnapRecordStartEvent and SnapRecordStopEvent among the unsupported APIs on Spectacles (2024). Those are the hooks a Lens would use to know
  about or trigger a capture, and they don't function on this device. So there's no way for the lens to export the drawing or post it itself.

  The route that does work is the device's own capture. The wearer presses capture on the glasses, and the recording includes everything they're seeing — the corpse, breathing and leaning. That footage goes to the Spectacles app and can be
  shared to Snapchat from there. It's a system-level feature, entirely outside your lens's control, which is also why it needs no work from us.

  Two things worth knowing about that, given what we've built:

  The drawing is procedural ribbon geometry rebuilt every frame, not an image — there's no bitmap to hand off even if an export API existed. Capture-as-video is the only form it can take.

  And it means the reveal already is the shareable moment. Someone recording will most likely start filming once the creature is alive, so the title card, the stroke-by-stroke draw-on and the lean are the beats that end up on Snapchat. That's
  an argument for drawOnSeconds being generous enough to film — 4.5 s currently, which is about right for a clip.

  One thing I couldn't establish: whether the Spectacles app offers a direct "share to Snapchat" button versus saving to camera roll first. The knowledge base didn't cover it and I'd only be guessing — worth a quick check on your own device,
  since it's a one-tap difference in the demo flow.

✻ Worked for 1m 5s

❯ is there a way to hide things from capture, like the replay arrow                                                                                                                                                                                 

  Called lens-studio (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The mechanism exists in Lens Studio — but whether Spectacles honours it is the real question. Checking.

  Called lens-studio (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes — the mechanism exists, and it's designed for exactly your case.

  Live Target vs Capture Target. These are set on the Scene asset. The docs describe the purpose almost verbatim as your question: "handy when you have UI elements or hints in the Lens that you want to hide in the final recorded snap, but still
  be present during the recording." Cameras render to a target; anything on a camera that only feeds the Live Target is seen by the wearer but absent from the capture.

  Overlay Target is the blunter version — "cannot be added to the final captured Snap" by definition. Put a camera on an Overlay Render Target and its contents are structurally excluded, with the side benefit of rendering at the device's native
  resolution, which is nicer for text and icons.

  How it would apply here. Your reveal already hides almost everything: at that point the only chrome is the restart circle, since the guides have faded, the rails are hidden and the title card is gone. So the change is small — move RevealPanel
  onto its own camera and layer feeding the Live Target only. The drawing itself stays on the main camera and captures normally. The same trick would cover the HUD rail and palette if you ever wanted a clean capture mid-turn.

  The caveat I can't resolve from here. All of that documentation is general Lens Studio, and Spectacles is explicitly a partial API surface — SnapImageCaptureEvent, SnapRecordStartEvent and SnapRecordStopEvent are on its unsupported list. I
  found nothing confirming that Spectacles capture honours the Live/Capture Target split, and it's plausible its capture is a straight recording of the display, in which case none of this applies. The knowledge base simply doesn't say.

  So: the feature is real, it's the right feature, and I'd want to confirm it works on Spectacles before building around it. Worth a test capture on device — record the reveal and see whether the restart circle appears in the result. If it
  does, this route is out and the alternative is behavioural: hide the circle after a few idle seconds and bring it back on a pinch, so the creature is alone for most of any recording.

  Want me to set up the camera and layer split so you can test it, or wait until you've checked what a device capture actually includes?

  Two design calls worth flagging:

  It skips the title card. "The Finished Piece" belongs to the moment the last player finishes, not to a replay the viewer asked for — so replay starts at the drawing, not the words.

  Replay is a pinch, restart stays a gaze dwell. Replaying costs nothing, so it doesn't need the deliberate hold; restarting destroys the drawing, so it keeps its 2.6× dwell. That split matches the rest of the lens — the palette and READY are
  pinches, DONE and restart are dwells.

  One bug I caught while wiring it: the reveal registers its gaze zone through a deferred callback, and a replay runs that path a second time, which would have stacked a duplicate playagain zone each time you replayed. It now clears zones
  before registering.

⏺ Here is the full inventory, then the theme read.

  Features

  Home
  - Title lockup: "Exquisite" upright serif, "Corpse" italic in cyan, flanking hairlines, logo mark above
  - PLAY button, plus two circular controls: mute and info (?)
  - Info card with the rules and the Surrealist history (Paris, 1925, "the exquisite corpse shall drink the new wine"), carrying its own PLAY so you never have to back out
  - Section count configurable 2 to 6, so the canvas splits to fit the group
  
  Placement
  - Canvas previews in front of you, then you pinch and drag it to wherever you want it in the room
  - Chrome stripped to one uppercase hint and LOCK HERE, so you judge the position against the canvas and nothing else
  - LOCK HERE commits it to world space. It stays put while people move around it
  
  Drawing a turn
  - Pinch to draw. The mark is cast along the ray from your head through the pinch point, so it lands where you see your fingers against the canvas, and the far corners stay reachable
  - Neon ribbon geometry built at runtime: five columns per stroke fading to transparent at the edges, so the glow is the vertex colour under an additive material. One draw call per band, no post pass
  - Strokes are clipped to your own band. You cannot draw outside it even if you try
  - The seam: the bottom 3.5 cm of the band above is all you inherit. Narrow on purpose
  - Cyan corner brackets sit just outside the active band to mark whose turn it is
  - Vertical HUD rail: player number, DONE, UNDO, mute
  - Vertical palette rail with per player colour, selected chip taking a thicker ring
  - Band prompt pinned above the canvas ("Draw a Head", "Draw a Body", "Draw Legs")
  - Gaze dwell weighted by consequence: UNDO is cheap, DONE ends your turn with no way back, NEW GAME destroys the whole drawing and holds longest

  The pass
  - Strokes are hidden before the pass panel is built, so there is no single frame where the outgoing player's drawing and the pass instruction are on screen together. The phase order is the anti cheat
  - "Pass to Player 2", then a pinch on READY

  The reveal
  - Four beats: a title card alone, a fade, the whole drawing inking itself back on line by line, then it comes alive
  - Face detection finds the eyes, mouth and head outline in whatever was drawn, then blinks them 
  - Per region idle motion: the head barely moves, the torso more, the legs more again, hair most of all
  - The whole figure sways as one rigid piece, rotating from its feet
  - It leans toward where you are standing
  - REPLAY and NEW GAME as pills below the canvas

  Audio
  - Four generated assets: a confirm blip, an undo whoosh, a reveal chime, a looping ambient bed
  - Mute reachable from the home screen and mid turn from the HUD

  Under the hood
  - Built throughout for an additive display, where black is absent light and alpha reads as brightness
  - Every panel scaled by its actual distance, so a six player canvas sitting further back reads at exactly the same apparent size as a three player one
  - Arm delays on every screen transition so one pinch cannot cascade through two buttons

  How it reads against "Connect"

  Connects people: this is the strongest claim, and it is the whole game. Exquisite Corpse is a 1925 parlour game whose entire point is that strangers at a table make one thing together without coordinating. The lens does not simulate that
  socially, it requires it: 2 to 6 people physically in a room, one pair of Specs, passed hand to hand. The glasses become the pencil going round the table. Nobody is playing alone.

  It is a communication workflow with the bandwidth turned down. Each player receives 3.5 cm of context and has to continue from it. That is a handoff with almost all the information stripped out, which is exactly why the result is funny, and
  it is also a fair caricature of how work actually gets passed between people. The game makes the lossiness visible instead of hiding it.

  It is spatial in a way a screen version could not be. The canvas is world locked, not head locked, so it is an object in the room that everyone shares rather than a display one person owns. You lock it to a table or a wall, people gather
  round it, and at the reveal the finished creature leans toward whoever is looking at it. The drawing acknowledges the room it is in.

  The one place it does not yet reach is platforms. It is deliberately single device and offline: no network, no Snapchat share, no second pair seeing the same canvas. Two obvious extensions sit right there if you want to widen the claim:
  sharing the finished piece to Snapchat, and SyncKit colocated multiplayer so several pairs watch the same canvas fill in live. Worth naming them as roadmap rather than implying they exist.

  If it helps to have this as a page you can send to teammates or judges, say the word and I will publish it.

✻ Cooked for 4m 50s

※ recap: You're building the Exquisite Corpse AR drawing game for Snap Specs; the reveal screen's REPLAY and NEW GAME pill buttons are done and saved. I last listed the lens features and mapped them to the "Connect" theme. Next, tell me if you 
  want that written up as a shareable page.

❯ am I able to drag the canvas around before locking to put it against a wall or on a table? It seems to just be free floating to face me, I am not sure what is better                                                                             

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)
  
⏺ Bash(sed -n '190,215p' ExquisiteCorpseCanvasController.ts; echo "=== faceViewerRotation ==="; sed -n '530,540p' ExquisiteCorpseCanvasController.ts)
  ⎿    /**
        * Re-derive the canvas pose from wherever the player has dragged the handle.
        * Called every frame while placing.
     … +35 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Short answer: you can move it but not orient it.

  The drag handle is translate only (setCanTranslate(true), rotate and scale both off, ExquisiteCorpseCanvasController.ts:163). While you drag, syncFromHandle re-aims the canvas every frame to face you from wherever you just put it, with the
  facing vector flattened so it stays perfectly upright (poseAt, line 210). So you can push it back toward a wall or down toward a table, but it will keep turning to face you rather than lying flat or going parallel to the wall. There is no
  tilt.

  At LOCK HERE the rotation freezes and it is genuinely world locked from then on. Walk around it and it stays put.
  
  Which is better

  Keep it vertical. I do not think table or wall mounting is worth building here, for three reasons:

  1. You are drawing a standing figure in horizontal bands, head at the top, legs at the bottom. An easel is the right metaphor. On a tabletop "up" is different for every person around it, so the player opposite you inherits a seam that is
     upside down from their point of view.
  2. The reveal animation is built on vertical throughout: the sway rotates about the figure's feet, the ripple gets stronger as you go down the body, and hair moves most. Flat on a table none of that reads.
  3. Snapping to a real wall needs surface detection, which is a live failure mode in whatever room you demo in. Free floating works anywhere.

  The gap that is actually worth closing

  Right now the orientation freezes at whoever placed it. Player 1 locks it facing themselves, then player 3 stands somewhere else and gets it at an angle, possibly quite oblique. The position should stay fixed (it is a shared object in the
  room, that is the point), but there is no reason the facing has to.

  The fix is small: re-aim the canvas once at the start of each turn, pivoting about its own anchor. It stays exactly where it was put, it just squares up to whoever is now wearing the glasses. That is one poseAt(anchor) call in showTurn plus
  caching the anchor at lock time. It suits pass and play much better than a fixed facing does, and it costs nothing in a demo.

  Want me to add that? I would also keep the per-turn re-aim off during the reveal, since the lean-toward-viewer effect there is already doing that job more subtly.

✻ Baked for 1m 2s

※ recap: We're building the Exquisite Corpse AR drawing lens; I just answered that the canvas can be dragged but not tilted, and recommended keeping it vertical. Next: your call on whether I add the per-turn re-aim so it squares up to each new 
  player.

❯ yes, add the fix as you recommended

⏺ Done and verified. Saved.

  What changed
  - ExquisiteCorpseCanvasController.reaimAtViewer() re-derives the canvas basis from the same centre, so the plane turns about its own anchor without moving
  - ExquisiteCorpseStrokeRenderer.refreshAllBands() re-projects every band mesh through the current basis. This was the necessary second half: band geometry is baked into world space at commit time, so without it the earlier bands would have
    kept pointing the old way and the reveal would have shown a fractured figure. The strokes themselves are stored in canvas-local cm, so a rebuild is all it takes
  - Both are called at the top of beginTurn, before anything derived from the canvas basis
  - New Re Aim Each Turn input on the ExquisiteCorpse component, default on, so you can switch back to the old fixed facing

  Verification. I locked the canvas at (0, 0, -170), moved the preview camera to (-90, 0, -70), about 42° round to the side, and advanced a turn. The before shot shows exactly the problem: the canvas skewed into a trapezoid with the prompt
  clipped off the top. After the turn advance it is square on again.

  The numbers confirm it is a rotation and not a move. Every panel's position expressed in canvas-local coordinates is bit-identical across the re-aim, while its world position shifted by tens of centimetres:

               canvas-local x, y, normal
               before                  after
  HUD frame    33.527  29.641  2.057   33.527  29.641  2.057
  HUD done     31.317  27.403  2.725   31.317  27.403  2.725
  Palette fr  -29.645  27.629  2.057  -29.645  27.629  2.057
  Palette t1  -31.940  33.976  2.725  -31.940  33.976  2.725

  Two things worth flagging:

  I could not test the stroke re-projection end to end, because the synthetic hand in the preview harness will not trigger the draw controller, so I could not lay down a stroke to carry across a turn. The mechanism is the same one updateLife
  already uses every frame at the reveal (rebuild from bandStrokes through toWorld), so I am confident in it, but it is worth one pass on device with an actual drawing before the demo.

  The pass panel itself still shows at the previous player's facing. Player 2 puts the glasses on, reads "Pass to Player 2" at whatever angle player 1 left it, and only when they pinch READY does everything square up. My change does not make
  that worse, but it does not fix it either. The clean fix is to pose the pass panel in front of the viewer rather than at canvas centre, since it is the one screen that is about the person and not about the drawing. Say the word if you want
  that too.

⏺ Rebalanced and saved. SFX 0.75 → 0.35, music 0.22 → 0.45, set in both the script default and the scene value so a recompile cannot quietly undo it.

  I measured the files first rather than guessing, and the assets turned out not to be the problem:

  AmbientBed     mean -19.1 dB   peak  -8.0 dB
  DoneConfirm    mean -17.6 dB   peak  -0.4 dB
  RevealChime    mean -17.2 dB   peak  -0.4 dB
  UndoWhoosh     mean -19.2 dB   peak  -0.4 dB

  Mean levels are within 2 dB of each other. The whole imbalance was in the volume settings, which had the cues sitting +10.6 dB above the bed. The new pair puts them at roughly equal mean level, with the cues still peaking about 5 dB above the
  bed. That is the part you want kept: the bed carries the continuous level and the cues poke through on their transients, so DONE and UNDO still register as feedback without startling anyone.

  Worth a listen on device before locking it in, since Specs speakers are far less bass-capable than desktop monitoring and a pad-heavy bed tends to thin out more than transient cues do. If it lands too quiet there, raise musicVolume rather
  than dropping the cues further, since the cues are close to the floor at 0.35 already.

  One thing I noticed and left alone: if musicVolume is 0 when the lens starts, the bed never begins playing, and unmuting later will not start it, because play(-1) only runs at awake. That only bites if you ship with the slider at zero, so it
  is not affecting you now, but say the word if you want it made robust.
